Well, I guess I’m here because I’ve been kind of stuck on how my face looks lately. It looks uneven to me, or sort of saggy.

It kind of pops back into my head.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your face and its appearance cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does bother me. He’s the one who put the mirror in, actually—he was just trying to be helpful because the old lighting was terrible—but now I keep noticing it, and it kind of sticks with me. It makes me feel self-conscious, even if part of me knows he doesn’t see what I’m talking about.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face, I think. It’s really the cheeks and around my jaw that I keep getting stuck on, like the skin looks uneven or kind of saggy to me.\n\nIt started after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror—my husband installed it, and honestly he was just trying to make the bathroom nicer, because the old light made everything look yellowish. I’m not really focused on other parts the same way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. He’s the one who put the mirror in, actually—he was just trying to be helpful because the old lighting was terrible—but now I keep noticing it, and it kind of sticks with me. It makes me feel self-conscious, even if part of me knows he doesn’t see what I’m talking about.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face, I think. I think I would be more satisfied, yeah… because that’s really what I keep getting caught on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, like it looks uneven or sort of saggy to me.

I mean, that’s kind of what I keep coming back to—the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and usually he’ll point things out if something’s actually off—like he’ll notice if a picture frame is crooked or if I missed a spot painting the trim—but with this he says he can’t see it. Still, when I look, that’s what I focus on. So I guess if that part looked perfectly even, maybe my face would seem normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if only the skin around your cheeks and jaw were changed to look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I think I would be more satisfied, yeah… because that’s really what I keep getting caught on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, like it looks uneven or sort of saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see it, which—he’s usually pretty observant, honestly, sometimes too observant about little things around the house—but I still see it when I look in that mirror. So if that part looked the way I wanted, I think I’d feel better about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things besides your appearance that you worry about or feel are not perfect?\n\nPatient: Um… besides my appearance? I don’t think everything, no… I mean, I like things neat, especially at work, because if a document is out of order it can turn into this whole annoying thing with the attorney asking where page three went, and then everyone’s digging through files.

But in my actual life? I don’t know that I’d say everything has to be perfect. Like my kitchen drawers are kind of a mess, honestly. My husband jokes about the junk drawer because it has batteries, receipts, takeout menus—well, the menus are old now because everyone uses apps—but anyway. It’s more that certain things catch my eye and bother me. The appearance thing feels different than, like, needing the whole world to be perfect.

Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and usually he’ll point things out if something’s actually off—like he’ll notice if a picture frame is crooked or if I missed a spot painting the trim—but with this he says he can’t see it. Still, when I look, that’s what I focus on. So I guess if that part looked perfectly even, maybe my face would seem normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if only the skin around your cheeks and jaw were changed to look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I think I would be more satisfied, yeah… because that’s really what I keep getting caught on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, like it looks uneven or sort of saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see it, which—he’s usually pretty observant, honestly, sometimes too observant about little things around the house—but I still see it when I look in that mirror. So if that part looked the way I wanted, I think I’d feel better about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things besides your appearance that you worry about or feel are not perfect?\n\nPatient: Um… besides my appearance? I don’t know. I mean, I worry about things being done right, like at work if a form is sloppy or if someone leaves papers in the wrong order, but that’s kind of normal for a legal office, you know? Everything has to be exact or someone gets annoyed.\n\nAt home I’ll notice little things too, like if the towels are folded weird, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking everything has to be perfect the same way. It’s not like the mirror thing. The face thing is what really pulls me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything in your life needs to be perfect, not just how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t think everything, no… I mean, I like things neat, especially at work, because if a document is out of order it can turn into this whole annoying thing with the attorney asking where page three went, and then everyone’s digging through files.\n\nBut in my actual life? I don’t know that I’d say everything has to be perfect. Like my kitchen drawers are kind of a mess, honestly. My husband jokes about the junk drawer because it has batteries, receipts, takeout menus—well, the menus are old now because everyone uses apps—but anyway. It’s more that certain things catch my eye and bother me. The appearance thing feels different than, like, needing the whole world to be perfect.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Perfectly in control of everything? I don’t think so… I mean, I like to know what’s going on, especially at work, because if something gets missed it can snowball. But everything I do? No, that sounds exhausting.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because I’ve been kind of stuck on how my face looks lately. We had this brighter mirror put in the bathroom—my husband thought it would be practical, you know, better lighting, and he’s always doing little projects around the house when he gets time—and ever since then I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. It looks uneven to me, or sort of saggy.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, which honestly makes me feel a little silly, but I still keep seeing it when I look in that mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your face a lot throughout the day, even when you're not looking in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess I do. It’s mostly since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. I’ll see my cheeks or my jaw in there and then later, even when I’m at work or doing something else, I’ll sort of remember how uneven or saggy it looked to me.\n\nMy husband keeps saying he can’t really see what I’m talking about, and he’s usually pretty honest—sometimes too honest, like with home projects and paint colors and all that—but I still keep thinking about it. It kind of pops back into my head.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your face and its appearance cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does bother me. I mean, I’m not crying all day or anything, but it’s upsetting because I’ll look in that brighter bathroom mirror and suddenly I’m focused on my cheeks and jaw, like the skin looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nAnd then my husband will say, “I don’t really see it,” which should make me feel better, I guess. He’s the one who put the mirror in, actually—he was just trying to be helpful because the old lighting was terrible—but now I keep noticing it, and it kind of sticks with me. It makes me feel self-conscious, even if part of me knows he doesn’t see what I’m talking about.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face, I think. It’s really the cheeks and around my jaw that I keep getting stuck on, like the skin looks uneven or kind of saggy to me.\n\nIt started after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror—my husband installed it, and honestly he was just trying to make the bathroom nicer, because the old light made everything look yellowish. But now every time I’m in there, I notice it. He says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so… I don’t know. I’m not really focused on other parts the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if the skin around your cheeks and jaw were perfectly even?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yeah. I mean, that’s kind of what I keep coming back to—the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and usually he’ll point things out if something’s actually off—like he’ll notice if a picture frame is crooked or if I missed a spot painting the trim—but with this he says he can’t see it. Still, when I look, that’s what I focus on. So I guess if that part looked perfectly even, maybe my face would seem normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if only the skin around your cheeks and jaw were changed to look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I think I would be more satisfied, yeah… because that’s really what I keep getting caught on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, like it looks uneven or sort of saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see it, which—he’s usually pretty observant, honestly, sometimes too observant about little things around the house—but I still see it when I look in that mirror. So if that part looked the way I wanted, I think I’d feel better about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things besides your appearance that you worry about or feel are not perfect?\n\nPatient: Um… besides my appearance? I don’t know. I mean, I worry about things being done right, like at work if a form is sloppy or if someone leaves papers in the wrong order, but that’s kind of normal for a legal office, you know? Everything has to be exact or someone gets annoyed.\n\nAt home I’ll notice little things too, like if the towels are folded weird, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking everything has to be perfect the same way. It’s not like the mirror thing. The face thing is what really pulls me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything in your life needs to be perfect, not just how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t think everything, no… I mean, I like things neat, especially at work, because if a document is out of order it can turn into this whole annoying thing with the attorney asking where page three went, and then everyone’s digging through files.\n\nBut in my actual life? I don’t know that I’d say everything has to be perfect. Like my kitchen drawers are kind of a mess, honestly. My husband jokes about the junk drawer because it has batteries, receipts, takeout menus—well, the menus are old now because everyone uses apps—but anyway. It’s more that certain things catch my eye and bother me. The appearance thing feels different than, like, needing the whole world to be perfect.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your face, besides the skin around your cheeks and jaw, that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else, no… it’s mostly that same area, the skin around my cheeks and jaw. Since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep catching it and thinking it looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nI mean, sometimes lighting is just awful—like in dressing rooms too, those overhead lights make everyone look tired—but in that mirror at home I keep seeing it. My husband says he doesn’t see what I’m talking about, or not really, but I still notice it. So no, not like my nose or eyes or anything. It’s that cheek and jaw skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be perfectly in order or done exactly right in most areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think in most areas, no. I mean, I can get particular about things, especially at work, because legal paperwork is so easy to mess up—one page out of place and suddenly everyone’s irritated and looking at me like I lost it. But that feels more like trying not to create a problem.\n\nAt home, honestly, things are not perfectly in order. My laundry sits in the basket sometimes, and there’s this pile of mail on the counter my husband keeps saying we should go through. So… no, I wouldn’t say most areas. Certain things bother me, but not everything has to be exact.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself checking or rechecking things in your daily life to make sure they are done correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say often. I mean, at work I do check things because with legal documents you kind of have to—if a signature page is missing or the exhibits are out of order, it becomes a whole thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to make my whole life flawless. There are things I like done a certain way, sure, but I wouldn’t say everything has to be perfect.

Like, my car right now has receipts in the cup holder and one of those grocery bags in the back seat I keep meaning to throw out, and then I just… don’t. So if I needed everything flawless, I’d be in trouble. But I guess I can get particular about some things at work, because legal paperwork is—well, you know, one wrong date and suddenly everybody’s making a whole production out of it. But beyond that, I haven’t really noticed that.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because I’ve been kind of stuck on how my face looks lately. We had this brighter mirror put in the bathroom—my husband thought it would be practical, you know, better lighting, and he’s always doing little projects around the house when he gets time—and ever since then I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. It looks uneven to me, or sort of saggy.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, which honestly makes me feel a little silly, but I still keep seeing it when I look in that mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your face a lot throughout the day, even when you're not looking in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess I do. It’s mostly since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. I’ll see my cheeks or my jaw in there and then later, even when I’m at work or doing something else, I’ll sort of remember how uneven or saggy it looked to me.\n\nMy husband keeps saying he can’t really see what I’m talking about, and he’s usually pretty honest—sometimes too honest, like with home projects and paint colors and all that—but I still keep thinking about it. It kind of pops back into my head.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your face and its appearance cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does bother me. I mean, I’m not crying all day or anything, but it’s upsetting because I’ll look in that brighter bathroom mirror and suddenly I’m focused on my cheeks and jaw, like the skin looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nAnd then my husband will say, “I don’t really see it,” which should make me feel better, I guess. He’s the one who put the mirror in, actually—he was just trying to be helpful because the old lighting was terrible—but now I keep noticing it, and it kind of sticks with me. It makes me feel self-conscious, even if part of me knows he doesn’t see what I’m talking about.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face, I think. It’s really the cheeks and around my jaw that I keep getting stuck on, like the skin looks uneven or kind of saggy to me.\n\nIt started after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror—my husband installed it, and honestly he was just trying to make the bathroom nicer, because the old light made everything look yellowish. But now every time I’m in there, I notice it. He says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so… I don’t know. I’m not really focused on other parts the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if the skin around your cheeks and jaw were perfectly even?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yeah. I mean, that’s kind of what I keep coming back to—the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and usually he’ll point things out if something’s actually off—like he’ll notice if a picture frame is crooked or if I missed a spot painting the trim—but with this he says he can’t see it. Still, when I look, that’s what I focus on. So I guess if that part looked perfectly even, maybe my face would seem normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if only the skin around your cheeks and jaw were changed to look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I think I would be more satisfied, yeah… because that’s really what I keep getting caught on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, like it looks uneven or sort of saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see it, which—he’s usually pretty observant, honestly, sometimes too observant about little things around the house—but I still see it when I look in that mirror. So if that part looked the way I wanted, I think I’d feel better about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things besides your appearance that you worry about or feel are not perfect?\n\nPatient: Um… besides my appearance? I don’t know. I mean, I worry about things being done right, like at work if a form is sloppy or if someone leaves papers in the wrong order, but that’s kind of normal for a legal office, you know? Everything has to be exact or someone gets annoyed.\n\nAt home I’ll notice little things too, like if the towels are folded weird, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking everything has to be perfect the same way. It’s not like the mirror thing. The face thing is what really pulls me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything in your life needs to be perfect, not just how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t think everything, no… I mean, I like things neat, especially at work, because if a document is out of order it can turn into this whole annoying thing with the attorney asking where page three went, and then everyone’s digging through files.\n\nBut in my actual life? I don’t know that I’d say everything has to be perfect. Like my kitchen drawers are kind of a mess, honestly. My husband jokes about the junk drawer because it has batteries, receipts, takeout menus—well, the menus are old now because everyone uses apps—but anyway. It’s more that certain things catch my eye and bother me. The appearance thing feels different than, like, needing the whole world to be perfect.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your face, besides the skin around your cheeks and jaw, that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else, no… it’s mostly that same area, the skin around my cheeks and jaw. Since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep catching it and thinking it looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nI mean, sometimes lighting is just awful—like in dressing rooms too, those overhead lights make everyone look tired—but in that mirror at home I keep seeing it. My husband says he doesn’t see what I’m talking about, or not really, but I still notice it. So no, not like my nose or eyes or anything. It’s that cheek and jaw skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be perfectly in order or done exactly right in most areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think in most areas, no. I mean, I can get particular about things, especially at work, because legal paperwork is so easy to mess up—one page out of place and suddenly everyone’s irritated and looking at me like I lost it. But that feels more like trying not to create a problem.\n\nAt home, honestly, things are not perfectly in order. My laundry sits in the basket sometimes, and there’s this pile of mail on the counter my husband keeps saying we should go through. So… no, I wouldn’t say most areas. Certain things bother me, but not everything has to be exact.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself checking or rechecking things in your daily life to make sure they are done correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say often. I mean, at work I do check things because with legal documents you kind of have to—if a signature page is missing or the exhibits are out of order, it becomes a whole thing. We had this one filing where someone used the wrong version of a document, and everybody was upset for days, so I guess that made me more careful.\n\nBut just in daily life… I don’t think I’m going around rechecking the stove or locks over and over, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’ll look back at an email before I send it, but I think most people do that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a big pattern of that outside normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you spend an excessive amount of time making sure other aspects of your life, beyond your appearance, are perfectly in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not beyond, you know, regular keeping up with things. I mean, sometimes if the kitchen gets messy I’ll get annoyed, but then I’ll also leave a mug in the sink for two days, so I’m probably not the best example of “perfectly in order.”\n\nAt work I try to keep files straight, but that’s more because if something gets misfiled it turns into this whole office drama, and then everyone’s whispering near the copier—actually our copier has been jamming constantly lately, which drives me crazy. But no, I don’t spend hours making the rest of my life perfect or anything. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be perfectly in control of everything you do?\n\nPatient: Perfectly in control of everything? I don’t think so… I mean, I like to know what’s going on, especially at work, because if something gets missed it can snowball. But everything I do? No, that sounds exhausting.\n\nHonestly, half the time I’m just trying to get through the day without forgetting my lunch on the counter or losing my reading glasses again. I bought one of those little chains for them and then misplaced the chain, which is kind of ridiculous. So no, I wouldn’t say I have to be perfectly in control of everything. Maybe certain things I want handled right, but not everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be excessively upset if something in your daily life, unrelated to your appearance, was not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not excessively. I might get irritated if something goes wrong—like if I forget to pay a bill on time or the printer at work eats half the packet right before someone needs it—but I usually just get annoyed and deal with it.\n\nActually, our office printer has this thing where it says there’s a jam, but there isn’t, and then everybody stands around opening all the little doors like we’re defusing a bomb. But, um… no, I wouldn’t say I fall apart if regular daily things aren’t perfect. I haven’t really noticed that about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling like other parts of your life, beyond how you look, need to be flawless?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to make my whole life flawless. There are things I like done a certain way, sure, but I wouldn’t say everything has to be perfect.\n\nLike, my car right now has receipts in the cup holder and one of those grocery bags in the back seat I keep meaning to throw out, and then I just… don’t. So if I needed everything flawless, I’d be in trouble. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a big pattern of that outside normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you spend an excessive amount of time making sure other aspects of your life, beyond your appearance, are perfectly in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not beyond, you know, regular keeping up with things. I mean, sometimes if the kitchen gets messy I’ll get annoyed, but then I’ll also leave a mug in the sink for two days, so I’m probably not the best example of “perfectly in order.”\n\nAt work I try to keep files straight, but that’s more because if something gets misfiled it turns into this whole office drama, and then everyone’s whispering near the copier—actually our copier has been jamming constantly lately, which drives me crazy. But no, I don’t spend hours making the rest of my life perfect or anything. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be perfectly in control of everything you do?\n\nPatient: Perfectly in control of everything? I don’t think so… I mean, I like to know what’s going on, especially at work, because if something gets missed it can snowball. But everything I do? No, that sounds exhausting.\n\nHonestly, half the time I’m just trying to get through the day without forgetting my lunch on the counter or losing my reading glasses again. I bought one of those little chains for them and then misplaced the chain, which is kind of ridiculous. So no, I wouldn’t say I have to be perfectly in control of everything. Maybe certain things I want handled right, but not everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be excessively upset if something in your daily life, unrelated to your appearance, was not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not excessively. I might get irritated if something goes wrong—like if I forget to pay a bill on time or the printer at work eats half the packet right before someone needs it—but I usually just get annoyed and deal with it.\n\nActually, our office printer has this thing where it says there’s a jam, but there isn’t, and then everybody stands around opening all the little doors like we’re defusing a bomb. But, um… no, I wouldn’t say I fall apart if regular daily things aren’t perfect. I haven’t really noticed that about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling like other parts of your life, beyond how you look, need to be flawless?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to make my whole life flawless. There are things I like done a certain way, sure, but I wouldn’t say everything has to be perfect.\n\nLike, my car right now has receipts in the cup holder and one of those grocery bags in the back seat I keep meaning to throw out, and then I just… don’t. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because I’ve been kind of stuck on how my face looks lately. It looks uneven to me, or sort of saggy.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, which honestly makes me feel a little silly, but I still keep seeing it when I look in that mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your face a lot throughout the day, even when you're not looking in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess I do. It’s mostly since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. It kind of pops back into my head.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your face and its appearance cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does bother me. I mean, I’m not crying all day or anything, but it’s upsetting because I’ll look in that brighter bathroom mirror and suddenly I’m focused on my cheeks and jaw, like the skin looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nAnd then my husband will say, “I don’t really see it,” which should make me feel better, I guess. He’s the one who put the mirror in, actually—he was just trying to be helpful because the old lighting was terrible—but now I keep noticing it, and it kind of sticks with me. But now every time I’m in there, I notice it. He says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so… I don’t know. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, like it looks uneven or sort of saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see it, which—he’s usually pretty observant, honestly, sometimes too observant about little things around the house—but I still see it when I look in that mirror. So if that part looked the way I wanted, I think I’d feel better about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things besides your appearance that you worry about or feel are not perfect?\n\nPatient: Um… besides my appearance? I don’t know. I mean, I worry about things being done right, like at work if a form is sloppy or if someone leaves papers in the wrong order, but that’s kind of normal for a legal office, you know? I don’t know that I’d say everything has to be perfect. Like my kitchen drawers are kind of a mess, honestly. My husband jokes about the junk drawer because it has batteries, receipts, takeout menus—well, the menus are old now because everyone uses apps—but anyway. It’s more that certain things catch my eye and bother me. The appearance thing feels different than, like, needing the whole world to be perfect.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your face, besides the skin around your cheeks and jaw, that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else, no… it’s mostly that same area, the skin around my cheeks and jaw. Since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep catching it and thinking it looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nI mean, sometimes lighting is just awful—like in dressing rooms too, those overhead lights make everyone look tired—but in that mirror at home I keep seeing it. My husband says he doesn’t see what I’m talking about, or not really, but I still notice it. So no, not like my nose or eyes or anything. It’s that cheek and jaw skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be perfectly in order or done exactly right in most areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think in most areas, no. But that feels more like trying not to create a problem.\n\nAt home, honestly, things are not perfectly in order. My laundry sits in the basket sometimes, and there’s this pile of mail on the counter my husband keeps saying we should go through. So… no, I wouldn’t say most areas. Certain things bother me, but not everything has to be exact.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself checking or rechecking things in your daily life to make sure they are done correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say often. I mean, at work I do check things because with legal documents you kind of have to—if a signature page is missing or the exhibits are out of order, it becomes a whole thing. But now every time I’m in there, I notice it. He says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so… I don’t know. I’m not really focused on other parts the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if the skin around your cheeks and jaw were perfectly even?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yeah. I mean, that’s kind of what I keep coming back to—the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and usually he’ll point things out if something’s actually off—like he’ll notice if a picture frame is crooked or if I missed a spot painting the trim—but with this he says he can’t see it. Still, when I look, that’s what I focus on. So I guess if that part looked perfectly even, maybe my face would seem normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if only the skin around your cheeks and jaw were changed to look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I think I would be more satisfied, yeah… because that’s really what I keep getting caught on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, like it looks uneven or sort of saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see it, which—he’s usually pretty observant, honestly, sometimes too observant about little things around the house—but I still see it when I look in that mirror. So if that part looked the way I wanted, I think I’d feel better about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things besides your appearance that you worry about or feel are not perfect?\n\nPatient: Um… besides my appearance? I don’t know. I mean, I worry about things being done right, like at work if a form is sloppy or if someone leaves papers in the wrong order, but that’s kind of normal for a legal office, you know? Everything has to be exact or someone gets annoyed.\n\nAt home I’ll notice little things too, like if the towels are folded weird, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking everything has to be perfect the same way. It’s not like the mirror thing. The face thing is what really pulls me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything in your life needs to be perfect, not just how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t think everything, no… I mean, I like things neat, especially at work, because if a document is out of order it can turn into this whole annoying thing with the attorney asking where page three went, and then everyone’s digging through files.\n\nBut in my actual life? I don’t know that I’d say everything has to be perfect. Like my kitchen drawers are kind of a mess, honestly. My husband jokes about the junk drawer because it has batteries, receipts, takeout menus—well, the menus are old now because everyone uses apps—but anyway. It’s more that certain things catch my eye and bother me. The appearance thing feels different than, like, needing the whole world to be perfect.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your face, besides the skin around your cheeks and jaw, that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else, no… it’s mostly that same area, the skin around my cheeks and jaw. Since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep catching it and thinking it looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nI mean, sometimes lighting is just awful—like in dressing rooms too, those overhead lights make everyone look tired—but in that mirror at home I keep seeing it. My husband says he doesn’t see what I’m talking about, or not really, but I still notice it. So no, not like my nose or eyes or anything. It’s that cheek and jaw skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be perfectly in order or done exactly right in most areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think in most areas, no. I mean, I can get particular about things, especially at work, because legal paperwork is so easy to mess up—one page out of place and suddenly everyone’s irritated and looking at me like I lost it. But that feels more like trying not to create a problem.\n\nAt home, honestly, things are not perfectly in order. My laundry sits in the basket sometimes, and there’s this pile of mail on the counter my husband keeps saying we should go through. So… no, I wouldn’t say most areas. Certain things bother me, but not everything has to be exact.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself checking or rechecking things in your daily life to make sure they are done correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say often. I mean, at work I do check things because with legal documents you kind of have to—if a signature page is missing or the exhibits are out of order, it becomes a whole thing. We had this one filing where someone used the wrong version of a document, and everybody was upset for days, so I guess that made me more careful.\n\nBut just in daily life… I don’t think I’m going around rechecking the stove or locks over and over, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’ll look back at an email before I send it, but I think most people do that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a big pattern of that outside normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you spend an excessive amount of time making sure other aspects of your life, beyond your appearance, are perfectly in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not beyond, you know, regular keeping up with things. I mean, sometimes if the kitchen gets messy I’ll get annoyed, but then I’ll also leave a mug in the sink for two days, so I’m probably not the best example of “perfectly in order.”\n\nAt work I try to keep files straight, but that’s more because if something gets misfiled it turns into this whole office drama, and then everyone’s whispering near the copier—actually our copier has been jamming constantly lately, which drives me crazy. But no, I don’t spend hours making the rest of my life perfect or anything. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be perfectly in control of everything you do?\n\nPatient: Perfectly in control of everything? I don’t think so… I mean, I like to know what’s going on, especially at work, because if something gets missed it can snowball. But everything I do? No, that sounds exhausting.\n\nHonestly, half the time I’m just trying to get through the day without forgetting my lunch on the counter or losing my reading glasses again. I bought one of those little chains for them and then misplaced the chain, which is kind of ridiculous. So no, I wouldn’t say I have to be perfectly in control of everything. Maybe certain things I want handled right, but not everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be excessively upset if something in your daily life, unrelated to your appearance, was not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not excessively. I might get irritated if something goes wrong—like if I forget to pay a bill on time or the printer at work eats half the packet right before someone needs it—but I usually just get annoyed and deal with it.\n\nActually, our office printer has this thing where it says there’s a jam, but there isn’t, and then everybody stands around opening all the little doors like we’re defusing a bomb. But, um… no, I wouldn’t say I fall apart if regular daily things aren’t perfect. I haven’t really noticed that about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling like other parts of your life, beyond how you look, need to be flawless?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to make my whole life flawless. There are things I like done a certain way, sure, but I wouldn’t say everything has to be perfect.\n\nLike, my car right now has receipts in the cup holder and one of those grocery bags in the back seat I keep meaning to throw out, and then I just… don’t. So if I needed everything flawless, I’d be in trouble. But I guess I can get particular about some things at work, because legal paperwork is—well, you know, one wrong date and suddenly everybody’s making a whole production out of it. But beyond that, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about details and order are as strong in areas of your life unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they’re as strong, no. I mean, I notice details because of my job—if a name is spelled wrong on a filing, that’s a headache for everyone—so I can get a little fussy there.\n\nBut at home? Not really. My kitchen drawers are not exactly organized by any grand system or anything. I did once buy those little dividers for utensils and then somehow the junk drawer swallowed half of them, so… no, I don’t think order in general takes over my life. I guess I’d need you to say what kind of details you mean, but I haven’t really noticed it outside of certain practical things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It looks uneven to me, or sort of saggy.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, which honestly makes me feel a little silly, but I still keep seeing it when I look in that mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your face a lot throughout the day, even when you're not looking in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess I do. I’ll see my cheeks or my jaw in there and then later, even when I’m at work or doing something else, I’ll sort of remember how uneven or saggy it looked to me.\n\nMy husband keeps saying he can’t really see what I’m talking about, and he’s usually pretty honest—sometimes too honest, like with home projects and paint colors and all that—but I still keep thinking about it. It kind of pops back into my head.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your face and its appearance cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does bother me. I mean, I’m not crying all day or anything, but it’s upsetting because I’ll look in that brighter bathroom mirror and suddenly I’m focused on my cheeks and jaw, like the skin looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nAnd then my husband will say, “I don’t really see it,” which should make me feel better, I guess. He’s the one who put the mirror in, actually—he was just trying to be helpful because the old lighting was terrible—but now I keep noticing it, and it kind of sticks with me. It makes me feel self-conscious, even if part of me knows he doesn’t see what I’m talking about.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face, I think. It’s really the cheeks and around my jaw that I keep getting stuck on, like the skin looks uneven or kind of saggy to me.\n\nIt started after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror—my husband installed it, and honestly he was just trying to make the bathroom nicer, because the old light made everything look yellowish. But now every time I’m in there, I notice it. He says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so… I don’t know. I’m not really focused on other parts the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if the skin around your cheeks and jaw were perfectly even?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yeah. I mean, that’s kind of what I keep coming back to—the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and usually he’ll point things out if something’s actually off—like he’ll notice if a picture frame is crooked or if I missed a spot painting the trim—but with this he says he can’t see it. Still, when I look, that’s what I focus on. So I guess if that part looked perfectly even, maybe my face would seem normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if only the skin around your cheeks and jaw were changed to look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I think I would be more satisfied, yeah… because that’s really what I keep getting caught on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, like it looks uneven or sort of saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see it, which—he’s usually pretty observant, honestly, sometimes too observant about little things around the house—but I still see it when I look in that mirror. So if that part looked the way I wanted, I think I’d feel better about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things besides your appearance that you worry about or feel are not perfect?\n\nPatient: Um… besides my appearance? I don’t know. I mean, I worry about things being done right, like at work if a form is sloppy or if someone leaves papers in the wrong order, but that’s kind of normal for a legal office, you know? Everything has to be exact or someone gets annoyed.\n\nAt home I’ll notice little things too, like if the towels are folded weird, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking everything has to be perfect the same way. It’s not like the mirror thing. The face thing is what really pulls me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything in your life needs to be perfect, not just how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t think everything, no… I mean, I like things neat, especially at work, because if a document is out of order it can turn into this whole annoying thing with the attorney asking where page three went, and then everyone’s digging through files.\n\nBut in my actual life? I don’t know that I’d say everything has to be perfect. Like my kitchen drawers are kind of a mess, honestly. My husband jokes about the junk drawer because it has batteries, receipts, takeout menus—well, the menus are old now because everyone uses apps—but anyway. It’s more that certain things catch my eye and bother me. The appearance thing feels different than, like, needing the whole world to be perfect.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your face, besides the skin around your cheeks and jaw, that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else, no… it’s mostly that same area, the skin around my cheeks and jaw. Since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep catching it and thinking it looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nI mean, sometimes lighting is just awful—like in dressing rooms too, those overhead lights make everyone look tired—but in that mirror at home I keep seeing it. My husband says he doesn’t see what I’m talking about, or not really, but I still notice it. So no, not like my nose or eyes or anything. It’s that cheek and jaw skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be perfectly in order or done exactly right in most areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think in most areas, no. I mean, I can get particular about things, especially at work, because legal paperwork is so easy to mess up—one page out of place and suddenly everyone’s irritated and looking at me like I lost it. But that feels more like trying not to create a problem.\n\nAt home, honestly, things are not perfectly in order. My laundry sits in the basket sometimes, and there’s this pile of mail on the counter my husband keeps saying we should go through. So… no, I wouldn’t say most areas. Certain things bother me, but not everything has to be exact.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself checking or rechecking things in your daily life to make sure they are done correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say often. I mean, at work I do check things because with legal documents you kind of have to—if a signature page is missing or the exhibits are out of order, it becomes a whole thing. We had this one filing where someone used the wrong version of a document, and everybody was upset for days, so I guess that made me more careful.\n\nBut just in daily life… I don’t think I’m going around rechecking the stove or locks over and over, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’ll look back at an email before I send it, but I think most people do that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a big pattern of that outside normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you spend an excessive amount of time making sure other aspects of your life, beyond your appearance, are perfectly in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not beyond, you know, regular keeping up with things. I mean, sometimes if the kitchen gets messy I’ll get annoyed, but then I’ll also leave a mug in the sink for two days, so I’m probably not the best example of “perfectly in order.”\n\nAt work I try to keep files straight, but that’s more because if something gets misfiled it turns into this whole office drama, and then everyone’s whispering near the copier—actually our copier has been jamming constantly lately, which drives me crazy. But no, I don’t spend hours making the rest of my life perfect or anything. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be perfectly in control of everything you do?\n\nPatient: Perfectly in control of everything? I don’t think so… I mean, I like to know what’s going on, especially at work, because if something gets missed it can snowball. But everything I do? No, that sounds exhausting.\n\nHonestly, half the time I’m just trying to get through the day without forgetting my lunch on the counter or losing my reading glasses again. I bought one of those little chains for them and then misplaced the chain, which is kind of ridiculous. So no, I wouldn’t say I have to be perfectly in control of everything. Maybe certain things I want handled right, but not everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be excessively upset if something in your daily life, unrelated to your appearance, was not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not excessively. I might get irritated if something goes wrong—like if I forget to pay a bill on time or the printer at work eats half the packet right before someone needs it—but I usually just get annoyed and deal with it.\n\nActually, our office printer has this thing where it says there’s a jam, but there isn’t, and then everybody stands around opening all the little doors like we’re defusing a bomb. But, um… no, I wouldn’t say I fall apart if regular daily things aren’t perfect. I haven’t really noticed that about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling like other parts of your life, beyond how you look, need to be flawless?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to make my whole life flawless. There are things I like done a certain way, sure, but I wouldn’t say everything has to be perfect.\n\nLike, my car right now has receipts in the cup holder and one of those grocery bags in the back seat I keep meaning to throw out, and then I just… don’t. So if I needed everything flawless, I’d be in trouble. But I guess I can get particular about some things at work, because legal paperwork is—well, you know, one wrong date and suddenly everybody’s making a whole production out of it. But beyond that, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about details and order are as strong in areas of your life unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they’re as strong, no. I mean, I notice details because of my job—if a name is spelled wrong on a filing, that’s a headache for everyone—so I can get a little fussy there.\n\nBut at home? Not really. My kitchen drawers are not exactly organized by any grand system or anything. I did once buy those little dividers for utensils and then somehow the junk drawer swallowed half of them, so… no, I don’t think order in general takes over my life. I guess I’d need you to say what kind of details you mean, but I haven’t really noticed it outside of certain practical things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about details and order are impacting areas of your life beyond just your appearance and work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not beyond work, anyway. Like I said, work kind of demands it, so I don’t know if that counts the same way.\n\nAt home I’m not, you know, lining things up or getting upset if the towels are folded wrong. Actually, my laundry is usually in that stage where it’s clean but living in the basket for three days, and then I pick through it like it’s a little clothing store on the floor. So… no, I haven’t really noticed details and order affecting other parts of my life. I guess if there’s a specific example you mean, maybe I’m not thinking of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concerns about your face are significantly more distressing to you than any other issues you might have in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, other things bother me, obviously, like work stuff or bills or my mother calling at the exact wrong time—she has this talent for it—but the face thing is what I keep getting stuck on.\n\nIt really started feeling worse after we put in this brighter bathroom mirror. I keep looking and thinking the skin around my cheeks and jaw looks uneven, or kind of saggy, and then I’m in there longer than I meant to be. My husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, which should probably make me feel better, but it doesn’t always. So yes, compared to other issues, that’s the one that upsets me more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know. I guess if I wasn’t so worried about my face, I’d probably have more room in my head, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure the details-and-order thing is really its own big problem for me.

Like at work, I’d still have to notice details, because that’s just… legal paperwork is picky by nature. One wrong date and suddenly everyone’s emailing in all caps. My purse right now probably has three old receipts and a granola bar wrapper in it, so I’m not exactly living like everything has to be perfect.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because I’ve been kind of stuck on how my face looks lately. We had this brighter mirror put in the bathroom—my husband thought it would be practical, you know, better lighting, and he’s always doing little projects around the house when he gets time—and ever since then I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. It looks uneven to me, or sort of saggy.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, which honestly makes me feel a little silly, but I still keep seeing it when I look in that mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your face a lot throughout the day, even when you're not looking in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess I do. It’s mostly since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. I’ll see my cheeks or my jaw in there and then later, even when I’m at work or doing something else, I’ll sort of remember how uneven or saggy it looked to me.\n\nMy husband keeps saying he can’t really see what I’m talking about, and he’s usually pretty honest—sometimes too honest, like with home projects and paint colors and all that—but I still keep thinking about it. It kind of pops back into my head.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your face and its appearance cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does bother me. I mean, I’m not crying all day or anything, but it’s upsetting because I’ll look in that brighter bathroom mirror and suddenly I’m focused on my cheeks and jaw, like the skin looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nAnd then my husband will say, “I don’t really see it,” which should make me feel better, I guess. He’s the one who put the mirror in, actually—he was just trying to be helpful because the old lighting was terrible—but now I keep noticing it, and it kind of sticks with me. It makes me feel self-conscious, even if part of me knows he doesn’t see what I’m talking about.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face, I think. It’s really the cheeks and around my jaw that I keep getting stuck on, like the skin looks uneven or kind of saggy to me.\n\nIt started after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror—my husband installed it, and honestly he was just trying to make the bathroom nicer, because the old light made everything look yellowish. But now every time I’m in there, I notice it. He says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so… I don’t know. I’m not really focused on other parts the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if the skin around your cheeks and jaw were perfectly even?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yeah. I mean, that’s kind of what I keep coming back to—the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and usually he’ll point things out if something’s actually off—like he’ll notice if a picture frame is crooked or if I missed a spot painting the trim—but with this he says he can’t see it. Still, when I look, that’s what I focus on. So I guess if that part looked perfectly even, maybe my face would seem normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if only the skin around your cheeks and jaw were changed to look the way you want?\n\nPatient: I think I would be more satisfied, yeah… because that’s really what I keep getting caught on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, like it looks uneven or sort of saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he doesn’t really see it, which—he’s usually pretty observant, honestly, sometimes too observant about little things around the house—but I still see it when I look in that mirror. So if that part looked the way I wanted, I think I’d feel better about my appearance.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things besides your appearance that you worry about or feel are not perfect?\n\nPatient: Um… besides my appearance? I don’t know. I mean, I worry about things being done right, like at work if a form is sloppy or if someone leaves papers in the wrong order, but that’s kind of normal for a legal office, you know? Everything has to be exact or someone gets annoyed.\n\nAt home I’ll notice little things too, like if the towels are folded weird, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking everything has to be perfect the same way. It’s not like the mirror thing. The face thing is what really pulls me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like everything in your life needs to be perfect, not just how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t think everything, no… I mean, I like things neat, especially at work, because if a document is out of order it can turn into this whole annoying thing with the attorney asking where page three went, and then everyone’s digging through files.\n\nBut in my actual life? I don’t know that I’d say everything has to be perfect. Like my kitchen drawers are kind of a mess, honestly. My husband jokes about the junk drawer because it has batteries, receipts, takeout menus—well, the menus are old now because everyone uses apps—but anyway. It’s more that certain things catch my eye and bother me. The appearance thing feels different than, like, needing the whole world to be perfect.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your face, besides the skin around your cheeks and jaw, that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else, no… it’s mostly that same area, the skin around my cheeks and jaw. Since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep catching it and thinking it looks uneven or kind of saggy.\n\nI mean, sometimes lighting is just awful—like in dressing rooms too, those overhead lights make everyone look tired—but in that mirror at home I keep seeing it. My husband says he doesn’t see what I’m talking about, or not really, but I still notice it. So no, not like my nose or eyes or anything. It’s that cheek and jaw skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be perfectly in order or done exactly right in most areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think in most areas, no. I mean, I can get particular about things, especially at work, because legal paperwork is so easy to mess up—one page out of place and suddenly everyone’s irritated and looking at me like I lost it. But that feels more like trying not to create a problem.\n\nAt home, honestly, things are not perfectly in order. My laundry sits in the basket sometimes, and there’s this pile of mail on the counter my husband keeps saying we should go through. So… no, I wouldn’t say most areas. Certain things bother me, but not everything has to be exact.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself checking or rechecking things in your daily life to make sure they are done correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say often. I mean, at work I do check things because with legal documents you kind of have to—if a signature page is missing or the exhibits are out of order, it becomes a whole thing. We had this one filing where someone used the wrong version of a document, and everybody was upset for days, so I guess that made me more careful.\n\nBut just in daily life… I don’t think I’m going around rechecking the stove or locks over and over, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’ll look back at an email before I send it, but I think most people do that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a big pattern of that outside normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you spend an excessive amount of time making sure other aspects of your life, beyond your appearance, are perfectly in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not beyond, you know, regular keeping up with things. I mean, sometimes if the kitchen gets messy I’ll get annoyed, but then I’ll also leave a mug in the sink for two days, so I’m probably not the best example of “perfectly in order.”\n\nAt work I try to keep files straight, but that’s more because if something gets misfiled it turns into this whole office drama, and then everyone’s whispering near the copier—actually our copier has been jamming constantly lately, which drives me crazy. But no, I don’t spend hours making the rest of my life perfect or anything. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be perfectly in control of everything you do?\n\nPatient: Perfectly in control of everything? I don’t think so… I mean, I like to know what’s going on, especially at work, because if something gets missed it can snowball. But everything I do? No, that sounds exhausting.\n\nHonestly, half the time I’m just trying to get through the day without forgetting my lunch on the counter or losing my reading glasses again. I bought one of those little chains for them and then misplaced the chain, which is kind of ridiculous. So no, I wouldn’t say I have to be perfectly in control of everything. Maybe certain things I want handled right, but not everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be excessively upset if something in your daily life, unrelated to your appearance, was not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not excessively. I might get irritated if something goes wrong—like if I forget to pay a bill on time or the printer at work eats half the packet right before someone needs it—but I usually just get annoyed and deal with it.\n\nActually, our office printer has this thing where it says there’s a jam, but there isn’t, and then everybody stands around opening all the little doors like we’re defusing a bomb. But, um… no, I wouldn’t say I fall apart if regular daily things aren’t perfect. I haven’t really noticed that about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling like other parts of your life, beyond how you look, need to be flawless?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to make my whole life flawless. There are things I like done a certain way, sure, but I wouldn’t say everything has to be perfect.\n\nLike, my car right now has receipts in the cup holder and one of those grocery bags in the back seat I keep meaning to throw out, and then I just… don’t. So if I needed everything flawless, I’d be in trouble. But I guess I can get particular about some things at work, because legal paperwork is—well, you know, one wrong date and suddenly everybody’s making a whole production out of it. But beyond that, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about details and order are as strong in areas of your life unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they’re as strong, no. I mean, sometimes if the kitchen gets messy I’ll get annoyed, but then I’ll also leave a mug in the sink for two days, so I’m probably not the best example of “perfectly in order.”\n\nAt work I try to keep files straight, but that’s more because if something gets misfiled it turns into this whole office drama, and then everyone’s whispering near the copier—actually our copier has been jamming constantly lately, which drives me crazy. But no, I don’t spend hours making the rest of my life perfect or anything. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be perfectly in control of everything you do?\n\nPatient: Perfectly in control of everything? I don’t think so… I mean, I like to know what’s going on, especially at work, because if something gets missed it can snowball. But everything I do? No, that sounds exhausting.\n\nHonestly, half the time I’m just trying to get through the day without forgetting my lunch on the counter or losing my reading glasses again. I bought one of those little chains for them and then misplaced the chain, which is kind of ridiculous. So no, I wouldn’t say I have to be perfectly in control of everything. Maybe certain things I want handled right, but not everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be excessively upset if something in your daily life, unrelated to your appearance, was not done perfectly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not excessively. I might get irritated if something goes wrong—like if I forget to pay a bill on time or the printer at work eats half the packet right before someone needs it—but I usually just get annoyed and deal with it.\n\nActually, our office printer has this thing where it says there’s a jam, but there isn’t, and then everybody stands around opening all the little doors like we’re defusing a bomb. I guess if there’s a specific example you mean, maybe I’m not thinking of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concerns about your face are significantly more distressing to you than any other issues you might have in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, other things bother me, obviously, like work stuff or bills or my mother calling at the exact wrong time—she has this talent for it—but the face thing is what I keep getting stuck on.\n\nIt really started feeling worse after we put in this brighter bathroom mirror. I keep looking and thinking the skin around my cheeks and jaw looks uneven, or kind of saggy, and then I’m in there longer than I meant to be. My husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, which should probably make me feel better, but it doesn’t always. So yes, compared to other issues, that’s the one that upsets me more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel significantly less concerned about details and order in your life if your appearance concerns were resolved?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I guess if I wasn’t so worried about my face, I’d probably have more room in my head, if that makes sense. But I’m not sure the details-and-order thing is really its own big problem for me.\n\nLike at work, I’d still have to notice details, because that’s just… legal paperwork is picky by nature. One wrong date and suddenly everyone’s emailing in all caps. But outside of that, I don’t know that I’m especially concerned with order. My purse right now probably has three old receipts and a granola bar wrapper in it, so I’m not exactly living like everything has to be perfect.\n\nSo… maybe I’d feel calmer overall if the appearance stuff wasn’t there, but I can’t say I’ve noticed some separate details-and-order concern that would disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or upset if you couldn't control or perfect something unrelated to your appearance?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a major way.
